=== FILE: 3 SIMPLE Things That Make Your Back Unbelievably Wide & Thick [7HhkVdpbjUQ].txt ===

 think back, I've done my first ever weighted one-armed pull up, and how outside my pull up training, I've made great strides in my back I've ready strgi by deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep deep at the very least that exercise selection really does come down to three things. Find a vertical pull you like, a row, and finally a heavy hip hinge. The simplicity of it all is beautiful. You're likely already doing all those things, but what matters the most is execution. And that's what I don't see done well very often. With all that in mind, how you develop your width should be intentional because regardless of which exercise you pick in the vertical plane, grip and elbow path are what matters the most. Because you see, width ultimately comes from three things, your upper lats, your lower lats, and that big old hunk of ribeye called the Terry's, otherwise known as the lats little helper. And how these three things get developed depends entirely on how you execute your movements. Now for those upper lats, it is my instinct to just recommend a good old-fashioned weighted neutral grip pull-up, particularly with the elbows kept in front and tight to the sides. Those are going to smash your upper lats to smithereens. You can't deny that. However, the honest truth is this. In that regard, pull-ups gave me the best return on investment when I could do a lot of them, say 20 or more, and add a lot of weight to them as well. So instead, I do think that neutral grip lat pull-downs will be a better option for most of you, but still immensely effective. Just missing out on some cool points, that's all. The truth is, lat pull-downs loading is more customizable than pull-ups, and even when you get really strong, you can add weight to the stack going beyond the full stack. So you can spam these bad boys at any point in your training career, whether you're new or advanced. But whichever one you pick, in terms of execution, other than keeping those elbows tight and in front of you, I'm also a big believer in adding in three-quarter reps to your routine to supplement full range of motion reps. They're fun, help you pump up your volume, and most importantly, help you keep a better mind-muscle connection with your back. How I suggest implementing this is pretty damn simple. Just try replacing a full day of vertical pulling with three-quarter reps, and let me know what you think. In terms of reps, your standard three sets of 8 to 12 with the double progression works great. But a fun mix-up is to do a heavy top set and then strip some weight for back offsets and more reps. Moving on down from the upper lats to the lower lats, these puppies are something that people treat like freaking unicorns, but they're pretty simple to hit as well. You can maintain their size pretty well with just about any back exercise that you enjoy, as long as you stay strong at it. But in terms of building them specifically, I've always loved rack chins. They leverage your body for a diagonal pull exactly like your favorite high to low pull machine, but with zero equipment needed, other than something to elevate your feet on and then a bar to hang off of, which can even be a barbell as you see me using here. Rack chins are easily loadable if you need to, you can do them anywhere, and they're accessible since you're only lifting a portion of your body weight. So if you can't do very many pull-ups, try these out. You're going to be able to bust out 10 or more, I bet. Cooper, a longtime friend and client of mine, absolutely loves these. For reference, he's the size of a moose, and here's him repping out 315 pounds on the incline press like air, so they're scalable for all experience levels. Don't be afraid to load these bad boys up when you can get past more than 12 reps or so in a row. Now, I'd be doing a disservice if I didn't also mention that I also love doing super wide grip pull-ups as well, but to be honest, they're not super accessible, and I just like rack chins better. In terms of implementing these with a specific rep range, do your normal rep range, but every once in a while, for fun, do a cluster set. I got this from world-renowned coach Josh Bryant, and they're a classic, engaging, and fun way to get a ton of work in in a small amount of time. You're going to do 10 to 12 reps to failure, rest a few breaths, bang out three to five more reps, and then rinse and repeat that for a few minutes. Enjoy the massive pump and all the productive work that you just got in on a small amount of time. Lastly, for the Terry's, the lats little helper, your best bet is a golden era dime, the behind-the-neck pull-down. Execution-wise, the key for me is to pull to only about my ears at most, and personally, I just pull until I bonk the bar off the back of my coconut. That's the perfect range of motion to isolate the Terry's, and actually a lot of lats too. Not only does this technique eliminate a lot of the momentum that we typically need to use to get that last little bit of range of motion, but it also spares the biceps and the forearms a lot of fatigue, which is great if you wanted to isolate those muscles, but also if your biceps or your elbows happen to be sore, it's a great way to get in some back work while also allowing those things to recover. The truth is, a lot of benefits come into play with back exercises once you start to shorten that end range of motion a little bit. It allows you to isolate and expose key properties that otherwise aren't part of the movements that we do. Now hold your horses. That doesn't mean don't have a form standard. You still want that upper arm to be at least parallel to the ground to effectively target your back, and try to keep those elbows in a straight path when you're pulling down. Don't let them scoop back and forth at the bottom to compensate for a lack of confidence in your mobility. Overall, behind-the-neck pull-downs are a great finisher after you've already done a larger back exercise. Just do them in a slightly higher rep range, and you're good to go. Moving on from width now to thickness, getting that thickness up to snuff was much more of a struggle for me than it was getting my back wide. The answer to that dilemma now being frustratingly simple. It doesn't matter what row you do, how much weight you use, or how well you follow the Dr. Mike textbook on Accenture Control, or even if you watched my video on how to do barbell rows, the final answer. Pausing, or at least attempting to pause, must. And if you don't do it, oh boy, I dare say, my friend, that you are wasting your time, and you might as well just do a vertical pull. In my experience, stretching your upper and your mid-back with a lot of weight, and making sure that your rows generally, in terms of textbook form, look pretty good, is not enough to grow your upper and mid-back as well as you could. Now how I discovered this is one, in eating a slice of humble pie, but two, it was through doing copious amounts of Kelso shrugs. Named after a famous and dearly departed weightlifting coach, Paul Kelso, they specifically isolate a squeeze in the upper back, all without taxing your lats or arms. And I started thinking to myself, man, what if I started squeezing the shit out of my rows too? The result? Pumps that I've never felt, fun that I've never experienced, and a depth to my back that I've never really had before. So I won't go on about pausing at the top of rows very much, just demanded that I punctuate the urgency in this video, no matter what type of row that you do. Strangely enough, I don't find that pausing at the bottom of vertical pulls offers the same benefits. They actually make the set somewhat worse, so just keep that in mind. It is a bad habit I do have myself, no one's perfect, but just try to do well. And lastly, for fellas that want a low equipment option, try out inverted rows. Now I personally used to hate them, I'm not gonna lie, because I never felt my lats while I did them. But now, I love them, because what I do realize is that they hit that mid-back in isolation. Whichever exercise sounds the most fun to you, but try that cue out at home, fellas. Lastly, finishing up with the lower back, I won't waste your time. Everybody knows hip hinges work your lower back well, but just like with the upper back and lats, it's all about the motion of the ocean, baby. In other words, it's not what you do, but how you do it that matters. And spinal erectors are loaded entirely by isometric contraction on your hip hinge, which basically means you're flexing your muscles in place. They're not dynamically moving through any range of motion, unless you want to snap your shit up. Now, I do like movements where your back does dynamically move, but those are loaded a lot more gradually and a lot lighter. But fret not, my friend, there's two things that we can do with standard hip hinges to make them more effective, starting with slowing the reps down. Where slowing the tempo down for most muscle groups is generally just a way to get more out of less weight. Doing it for your lower back increases the total amount of time your low back is isometrically contracting. And two, you're going to stop your reps when your back is parallel to the ground or even a little bit lower than that. Your back gets more and more challenged the deeper you go. That's pretty much why we're doing that. You have what's called an active hinging range, which is the deepest that you need to go to basically stretch your hamstrings and your glutes. We're going below that so that we could further challenge our back. Now, both of these things working in tandem will reduce your working loads quite substantially, but the trade-off is your lower back is going to feel a lot stronger, get a lot more pumped, and overall, you'll feel a lot more solid. For those that are just starting out or that have never done something like this before, do one day of hinging in your active hinging range with a normal tempo. That's going to be to push performance and build strength, and then have another day where you slow down and extend that range of motion a bit.  I can still, I can still remember vividly how fired up I got as a kid when I got home and there was a new episode of my favorite show waiting for me or a really good part of my game for me for me to come back to. And lifting weights is one of only a few hobbies that as an adult brings me back to that. So it always amazes me how the gym is romanticized as this place of suffering when it's actually an amazing place to pick yourself up. No matter what, you can always hit the gym, get a sick pump, and immediately feel like a million bucks, no matter how bad your mood was before. Ladies, gentlemen, I got a Think back, try to remember the best training session that you've ever had in your entire life. The skin splitting pump, the PRs, one of the most consistent ingredients in making this happen boils down to something you've heard people say a million times before. Just get in there and do something. Now, while this is helpful advice on its own, my first tip for today adds that we should also aim to make that something feel good. Because ultimately, forcing yourself through something shitty just because you want a good outcome ironically leads to worse results. And if you just aim to have fun and make the exercises feel great, this is particularly helpful if you're someone that has trouble separating yourself from the outcome and expectations. In that regard, for me personally, I recently tried a few things with some of my favorite exercises with the goal of making them feel awesome. That led to some cool PRs and discoveries that I'd like to share with you guys at home too. Starting off strong with the channel favorite, Weighted Dips. Folks are always looking for ways to make these bad boys feel better, making this the perfect first step on our journey. As always, I'm overjoyed to share that I hit a volume PR of 28 total reps with three plates on ass to grass weighted dips after not even doing them for a couple weeks. Now, while this was a cool PR, I really want to share how I did it so that you can hit a PR at home too. The big game changer on top of having really good fundamentals with form was checking for stability because I can sometimes lose bracing and stability at the bottom of my reps, particularly on those really hard sets when pushing things to the limit. So during my warmups, I experimented with tucking my chin down and keeping it there because I'm a big believer in the idea that where the head goes, the body follows. You want to keep your back straight on hip hinges, you keep your neck straight. Oldest trick in the book. And I just wondered if the same thing would apply to my dips, which it did, thankfully. Overall, I'm trying to buy some more tensed up, rigid, braced body posture. And balling my chin up into my pecs helped a lot with enforcing that across my entire body. It's the smallest, stupidest thing in the world. But my abs stayed crunch and braced. My hips stayed back. And overall, I was locked into a great position to crank out rep after rep, which bada bing, bada boom led to the PR. But more importantly, it also helped out with the consistency of the range of motion. Since I could visually see where I was hitting depth, so my three sets were also very consistent in that regard as well. Now, for you fellas at home, if that chin tucking cue sounds at all interesting to you, go ahead and try it out. Let me know what you think. And if you're interested, check out this video I made on dip form fundamentals that'll really help that cue thrive after you finish watching this video. Ladies and gentlemen, moving from upper body to the legs, take a leap of faith with me because I have a way to make the more boring parts of leg training a lot more fun. And what better of a way to talk about legs than with horses? As a kid, horses in video games and in pop culture were just as cool to me as the big jacked superheroes and fighters that I love. While they're mostly known for their loyalty, obviously, we love them because of their powerful physical traits, chief among them being the fact that they look like open bodybuilders and have massive thigh muscles. Now, there's some leg day motivation. We know it's something we need to do. We know it's like eating our vegetables. But there's a difference between just eating your vegetables because you know you should and making an effort to make them delicious. Now, the late great John Meadows once said in an old video that hamstring curls are one of the most important leg exercises that you can do. Mostly because people always lack development back there. I think the problem is, is I really, truly don't think people push the leg curls hard enough. They don't put enough intensity into them. Now, we all do hamstring curls, but there's two things missing in most people's programs. One, emphasis. And two, a good standard of form. And despite stiff-legged deadlifts being awesome, I do them all the time. Hip hinges alone are not going to get you the hamstring development that you want, unfortunately. And once I heard that, I made it a point to have extremely strong hamstring curling strength on every variation. Lying, standing, seated. But my problem, nonetheless, is that when my hamstrings fatigued, I had a really bad habit of overly engaging my lower back to kickstart the weight and add reps to the logbook that probably shouldn't have been there. Further amplified by the fact that I use extra padding for more range of motion in the stretch position. Which is good for that, but ironically can make it even easier to kick your lower back into it. Now, the first solution that I thought of, super simple. Just stop training legs. But I did feel my hamstrings work a ton harder. And the weights were still substantial enough to be challenging. And here's the lesson for you, the golden tidbit. Most of the time, with any cue that you try, it shouldn't require you to strip away tons and tons of weight for you to improve the stimulus to fatigue ratio or make the exercise feel better. A decent decrease is normal. But if you go from using 200 pounds to using 50 pounds, we're crossing into gimmick territory, I'm afraid. Since I didn't have to strip back that much weight, I've actually built up to a little bit more weight than I was using before, which was really cool. You don't always need to increase weight or reps or even rep quality every time you go to the gym to have a good workout that builds muscle. Focus on doing your work and doing it well because performance is going to fluctuate anyway, baby, which is where step two comes into play. Give yourself a range of rep totals to hit at a given weight. Have the base be however many reps you can do on your worst day and your ceiling is what you can do on a great day. For example, let's say you're doing three sets of five to eight. In the long term, you're naturally going to be trying to max out this rep range with form that trashes your muscles. Now, there's a damn good reason why we work within these parameters and not just try to add reps every time because for one, progress isn't linear in the first place. Now for two, hitting one workout where you have less reps doesn't mean that there's an issue. And three, hitting a weight once for a lot of reps doesn't mean anything because you can grow from that exact performance multiple times before you truly master the weight, which finally leads us to step three, simplest of them all. Here's just where you adjust the weight up or down or where you address your recovery between sessions. First, in terms of lowering the weight or addressing your recovery, hitting lower reps once just means your performance fluctuated. Next, in terms of adding weight, if you're consistently hitting or surpassing the top end of your rep range, bada bing, bada boom, you add some weight. Meaning on one hand for newer lifters, you're going to add weight fast because you level up faster on account of being lower leveled. Like we mentioned now, for folks who've been training for a while, you'll hang out there longer before you add weight because you level up a little bit slower. And there you have it, folks.  Hey, wanna listen to some tunes? Hey, wanna listen to some tunes? Cable pullovers are a back exercise that works amazingly well for almost everyone, especially people that say they never feel anything in their back. They're the next best thing to the Nautilus pullover, which is universally loved and one of the best exercises for your back, and it's legendary within the lifting community for a reason, because anyone that does it, they get amazing results from it. But it's a very rare machine, so most people end up doing dumbbell pullovers instead, but in my opinion, if you like the dumbbell pullover, you'll love the cable version of the same movement, because it takes all the good parts of a dumbbell pullover and improves them substantially. The general benefits of a pullover, other than the fact that they look extremely cool, are one, they work your lats without stressing your biceps or your elbows. Two, they're a great exercise for improving thoracic mobility, so essentially your ability to extend and flex your spine. Three, they're a great accessory for exercise with regards to rowing and vertical pull, so that's your weighted pull-ups, your rows, your seal rows, things of that nature. The thing with the dumbbell pullover, though, is that it lacks tension in almost the entire range of motion, so although it does have a really nasty stretch at the bottom, pretty much only that very end range of the stretch portion is gonna be what you're actually feeling, and everything else other than that is weightless. You can also have a little bit of torso movement to help you complete the rep. That's smart, and you should do that because it smooths out the rep, but the cable pullover is automatically standardized, so you don't have to rely on standardizing how much you move your torso, pretty much. The way I look at that barbell row comparison is that you can row the same amount of weight for the same number of reps and have an entirely different looking back, just depending upon your form. But on something like a cable pullover, every rep and every weight increase will correlate better for most people, just because, like I said, it's easier to standardize. Now, that isn't a case against rowing motions. I probably like them more than most, but I also think it's a good idea to supplement our larger exercises with more precise ones, like the cable pullover or the chest supported row is another one that I like. Now, in terms of how you set up the cable pullover, there's a lot of ways to do this exercise. You set up the cable pullover really simple, set up a slight incline on your bench, and then raise the cable stack so that it's at a similar angle to your bench. I saw a few different angles when I researched the movement, but after trying them all, I like this one the most, and I got that one from the hypertrophy coach. He made a really good video on them. So for the form, though, it's really simple as well. You let your back stretch at the top. You want to imagine your shoulder blades pulling up, and then you pull your shoulder blades down to start the rep. And then with a little bend in your arm, you just put your arms to your side, rinse and repeat. It's super easy. Now, I like to keep my chest up as well since it makes the movement feel more stable for me, and I think it will for you too. Your arms are naturally going to stay at your sides as well, but just be cognizant that this should stay that way and try not to compromise your form to grind out those extra reps. Rest in peace to the greatest of all time, but I learned this from John Meadows years ago, but typically the closer you can keep your arms to your sides and kind of dig them into your hips, the better your lats will recruit. I feel it light up when I do these in a really good way. That brings us in how to program them. It's also pretty simple. You just throw in two hard sets after you do your rowing or your pulling just to drive some extra volume into your lats. I like to do them after I've already done whatever main tricep press that I want to do. Like I said, they do work the longer your triceps a bit, so you may come in pre-fatigued if you put them before your tricep work. It's just something to keep in mind. It's not that way for everybody. Experiment for yourself and see how it works for you, but that's a good rule of thumb. But with regards to that extra volume for our back, sometimes our back can get in some more quality work, but our elbows or biceps are too tired to get it done. And cable pullovers are perfect there because they don't require you to use your biceps or any elbow fatigue at all. Doing weighted calisthenics in addition to your barbell work will build up that ability to be able to do more benching later on in your journey as well, in case you did want to get more specific. Remember, this is all possible due to weighted calisthenics being easier to recover from than its barbell exercise counterpart. This is a strategy that you actually see a lot in high-level powerlifting. The same thing can apply to all barbell rows or any weighted calisthenics exercise in its counterpart. Our journey to implementing practical tips into our programs is going to bring us now to the land of leg days. This is the day of the week that most of us either loathe with a deep, indescribable hatred, or relish the very most out of any training day. Now, in my humble opinion, I think there's a primitive part of each of us that can enjoy a good old-fashioned leg workout. The problem is that most of us do exercises that we either hate or we aren't built well for. It doesn't matter if you know vegetables are good for you at the end of the day if you hate eating them because they're soggy, microwaved garbage. Check out my Berserk Method video if you want to learn how to cook. All jokes aside, though, fellas, I am a big believer in improving our general ability to squat and to hinge, but that only takes but so much training economy. To get the ball rolling, I like to fill in volume with more stable, generally applicable leg exercises so that we can do movements that feel better, we can push harder, and that work better with our own unique builds. Chief among these things being whatever leg machine you happen to have at your gym, whether it's a leg press, a hack squat, belt squat, pendulum squat, whatever your gym has. A simple way I like to implement these into people's programs is to just simply have one day where they squat, and then one day where they use all their machine work. Working on your ability to squat well for your build, keeping yourself more upright, keeping your hips out of the lift, and knees over your toes hones the skill of generally being able to get more out of leg exercises. We take all that and then apply it to our machine exercises, and bada-bing, bada-boom, we got a one plus one equals three. If you want a really simple teaching tool for forcing yourself to squat well for your particular build, heel elevated zombie squats are a great place to start. This combination of more efficient squat work along with more surgical machine squatting work is like the peanut butter and jelly of leg training. You'll feel way more confident. Your back will be fresher, which is pretty huge as well, but most importantly, you'll be able to just focus on pushing hard. Which brings us to our next way that we can implement this strategy. This next step, I dare say, is a crazy thing to consider if you love back squatting. But once you understand good squat mechanics for your build, unless there's a specific desire to either have a back squat in your program or train it for powerlifting, I'm actually a big fan of doing only stable leg machines on both days that you train legs. Because all things being equal, you can push your legs harder on a hack squat or a leg press than you can on a back squat because ultimately your back isn't getting recruited at all. Being able to push your legs as hard as possible without your lower back limiting you is a pretty good benefit that can stack up over years to grow your wheels that much more. I'm also a big believer in making leg day training as simple as possible. So the success is measured by applying some common sense progression along with a lot of hard work and not having to follow an intricate workout where we have a million cues that we need to keep in mind. That's my ideal. And lastly, a really big reason why I prefer to do this as well is because machine exercises are more forgiving if you redline them when compared to barbell exercises. For similar reasons, they don't recruit your back. Now, some of y'all might be wondering where hinging falls into play with this. And don't worry, there's nothing better than a good old fashioned Romanian deadlift or stiff leg deadlift for building up your lower back, glutes and hammies. The most common thing that I prescribe here is just a simple pause at the bottom. It makes every rep grow you a little bit better and helps you progress on a given weight for longer before adding more. The only other thing that I prescribe for hinges is actually a Smith RDL. And the reason being is that a lot of people have trouble keeping RDLs or stiff leg deadlifts in their hamstrings. If you use a Smith machine, the bar's fixed path will keep it exactly where it needs to be, which is close to your shins. Bro splits are like the redheaded stepchild of training programs. There's thousands of videos calling them the worst thing ever and with good reason. Internal versus external cues are basically the difference between internal cues being what you can feel and think about that others can't necessarily see and external cues being what you can't actually see. So for example, an internal cue would be your bench pressing. You can feel that your hand is not pressured evenly on both sides, but it looks on the outside that you're gripping the bar evenly. That's something that you feel. Also, how you brace is something that you can really only feel, especially if you got a shirt on. How something feels in your joints with regards to how you're positioned is also internal. External is pushing your knees forward on squats, pausing longer on presses, things of that nature. But leg press is pretty simple other than that. Lock in, push the sled. My program, Conquest, is going to cover a progression model for you to follow. But in short, you can go ahead and treat this lift like a serious strength movement, just like the back squat. You're going to get far better gains out of it when you think about it that way. Stiff leg deadlifts are similarly customizable. But let's start with the benefits of any variation that you choose to do. And I have a shocking statistic to share with you. 9 out of 10 natural or enhanced bodybuilders pick stiff legs or RDLs or something similar over conventional deadlifts when it comes to the hip hinge that they perform. These are the guys that make money from being jacked and likely have tried out a lot of different tactics. And I believe success leaves clues in this case. The reason being is multifactorial. So for one, stiff legs are going to work your hamstrings better than conventional. They're going to work your back way better due to putting your back at a bigger disadvantage mechanically. And they're also going to sacrifice a good bit of that leverage game that you need to play. And overall, you're going to be lifting a lot less weight, meaning that every set of stiff leg is going to work you better while wiping you out less. And the only tradeoff is that you're not working your quads at all because you're not bending at the knee and you're getting less traps because it's less overall weight stretching your traps, which is a pretty fair tradeoff considering that we're working the lower body better for the most part. Form wise, you can stick your feet out a little bit wider and overall have a slightly wider stance to work more adductors. Or you can bring your feet in a little closer than shoulder width and point those toes forward to work the hamstrings more and use less load overall than even the wider stance version that we just talked about. Regardless of what stance you choose, when it comes to working the hamstrings and adductors, I have found that focusing on the eccentric or the lowering phase, in other words, is more important than a lot of other muscle groups, almost more so than locking out all the way. In fact, when I cut my brain off and focus mostly on just feeling tension in the muscle and I watch back my footage, I discover funnily enough that I'm cutting out the top bit of the range of motion. But to conclude, deep ass to ankles leg press and stiff leg deadlifts alone are going to give you most of your leg mass by themselves. But there's a strong argument for deficit leg extensions and hamstring curls being an even better duo. To be honest though, I wouldn't be able to get fired up about doing those two as my main performance metrics at all. Maybe the hamstring curls, but the leg extensions, while effective, I don't think are as cool as a leg press. That's all she wrote though, fellas. First and foremost, you never get stale doing this because the weight and rep ranges are always changing. On the flip side, by the end of a linear program, you also hit those medium and lower reps, which are distinctly good at strengthening your tendons and joints. Don't argue over what set and rep range to use. Just use them all. They all have their benefits. First and foremost, with regards to what you're watching now, pause your overhead presses at the bottom, okay? That can be the number one thing that differentiates you between hitting a PR and not hitting a PR because the thing with overhead presses is that rep strength tends to not correlate well to higher intensity strength, meaning if you can do 10 reps with 70%, that may not correlate how you would think to a one rep max if you were to have paused it, more so than a bench press. That alone will help you if you've hit a plateau in your shoulder press training. Pause your shoulder presses at the bottom for a solid one count, if not for a little bit longer in your head. So count starting from zero. So if you're doing a one count, go zero, one. Another piece of advice that I could give you is to hold tension at the bottom. I've talked a little bit about this in prior training vlogs, but one thing that I want to do to expand upon that is, is that I find that a lot of people, especially when they're doing higher rep sets, what a lot of folks will do is what I like to call a dip and go or a smash and dash. And what that refers to is this. When you unrack overhead press, your bar is going to be at about the bottom of your chin at rest. And a really cool technique that you can do is instead of pressing from your chin, pretty much from where the bar is at rest, you can dip the bar down to your collarbone and then use that momentum to explode back up to lift more weight or hit more reps. What that does is create a stretch reflex. And that's a gigabrain way of saying, look, we're going to create some momentum so that we can move the weight without using our muscular strength. And that's really good for getting your reps up, like I said. But when it comes to training your true strength, that weakness will start to expose itself as the weights get heavier and heavier. And what it does, unfortunately, is it creates a false sense of confidence. So you're looking at your training, you're saying, hey, I can do 200 pounds for 10 reps. I'm going to be able to do 250 pounds for three reps. Easy peasy. And your expectations aren't met because your foundation wasn't laid effectively. I was feeling pretty juiced today because that threat of imminent divorce made me like, man, I have to just boost my strength by 20% from sheer willpower alone. I just have to make some gains appear out of nowhere. So I did, instead of the 140 that Sam had programmed for me, I did 155. I said, hey, look, man, I did 140 and it just felt a little easy. I'm going to move up. And I still got about a set of 10, I think that was. And then a set of eight afterwards. I also superset the behind the neck press with some behind the neck pull-ups. Usually this would be behind the neck pull-downs, but there was a rotation of like five different dudes using the lat pull-down. And where usually I would just be like, hey, bro, you mind if I work in? Instead, what I have here is just this rotation of five sweaty dudes that are just freaking going to war on that pull-down for some reason, just leaving all kinds of sweat stains on the seat. I don't know if I'm going to get swamp ass if I sit down in this chair. I don't know if this will create some sort of mutant virus that will infect everybody, bro. And on top of that, like five different dudes, I'm not going to be able to work into this rotation and not be waiting like six minutes between sets. So the alternative was to do some behind the neck pull-ups. It's not a big deal. I'll go back to doing the behind the neck pull-downs next week, hopefully. One other change that I made to this day in the middle of the training cycle was instead of doing all shoulders and then all back and then all triceps and then hitting some dips at the end, which is what Sam laid out for me originally, I started staggering my exercise order so that I would be doing press, pull, press, pull, and I would superset my second press and my second pull. There's a couple of reasons for that. First and foremost, I just find from a shoulder health perspective, it felt fine to just do all shoulders and then all back because I'm doing all the same work anyway. You're getting that benefit of, hey, do X amount of pulling for X amount of pressing. But what I found is that that benefit is significantly magnified if you stagger the exercises instead. And then from a performance perspective, I'm getting rest on my pressing muscles while I'm doing my pulls and I'm getting rest with my pulls while I'm doing my presses. And all things being equal, that rest is going to create an environment for more performance. And more performance means, again, all things being equal, more strength, more size development. This was a more significant consideration to make with my shoulders just because they are relatively behind the curve. So like my front and my side delts. But when it comes to my back, it's relatively ahead of the curve, which I wanted to keep that way. I think that if you have a relatively ahead of the curve back compared to your pressing muscles, it's going to create a better environment for shoulder health. And all things being equal, it's going to leave you a stronger lifter. What I'm really fired up about is that I was able to increase my shoulder press without putting on any body fat, which is what I set out to do from the beginning because how my physique looks is important to me. You want to look good. That's half the reason why any of us are even lifting weights in the first place. A goal that I have for myself this year on top of like my strength goals is to just see how much muscle and strength I can put on on my key performance metrics to like my main lifts while staying at a lean body fat percentage. Because I'll tell you what, one of my genetic advantages is that I'm able to build muscle at a relatively leaner body fat percentage than most people can at my level of training advancement. Finishing up the best rear delt exercise known to man, the reverse pec deck. We're going to roll right into triceps. I did a form and weight reset on my overhead extensions using my Omni bar just because I felt like I wasn't getting as much out of the movement as I did at first when I first started doing them.
